{"parentUuid":null,"isSidechain":true,"promptId":"7ce8881e-5fa8-4b31-a62a-9ff4d72be4e8","agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":"Deep internet research (use WebSearch + WebFetch extensively) on the STATE OF THE ART in AUTONOMOUS AI OFFENSIVE SECURITY / AI penetration-testing agents, as of 2025-2026. I'm designing a 1-of-1 fused tool and need to know exactly what exists, how it works, its published results, and — critically — its LIMITATIONS (the whitespace we can own).\n\nResearch and report on each of these (and any others you find), with SPECIFICS (architecture, techniques, published results/benchmarks, funding, open vs closed):\n1. **XBOW** — the autonomous pentester that topped a HackerOne US leaderboard. Architecture, how it validates findings, results, limitations.\n2. **Strix** (usestrix/strix) — the open-source one we're integrating; note how it's positioned vs others.\n3. **DARPA AIxCC (AI Cyber Challenge)** — the winning systems (e.g. Team Atlanta \"Atlantis\", Trail of Bits \"Buttercup\", Theori, etc.). What techniques won — the combination of LLM agents + fuzzing + symbolic execution + program analysis for autonomous vuln discovery AND patching. This is the deepest technical frontier — dig in.\n4. **Google Project Zero \"Big Sleep\" / Naptime** — LLM agent finding real 0-days in real software. Approach, results.\n5. **CAI (Cybersecurity AI)**, **PentAGI**, **HackingBuddyGPT**, **Nebula**, **PentestGPT** — open-source AI pentest agents; architectures + limits.\n6. **Commercial autonomous pentest / offensive**: Horizon3.ai NodeZero, Mindgard (AI red-teaming), RunSybil, Terra Security, Zap/others. What they claim, how they validate.\n7. **AI red-teaming of AI systems** (prompt injection, agent attacks): Microsoft PyRIT, Garak, Meta CyberSecEval, Anthropic/OpenAI red-team frameworks.\n\nFor EACH, capture: (a) core architecture (single-agent vs multi-agent, LLM + what tools, sandbox model), (b) how it VALIDATES a finding (does it re-execute/prove, or trust the LLM? — this is the key differentiator), (c) governance/authorization model (scope enforcement, is it hard or prompt-level?), (d) provenance/evidence (any cryptographic signing? reproducible PoCs?), (e) sovereignty (cloud-only vs local/self-hosted), (f) published effectiveness numbers, (g) key LIMITATIONS.\n\nEND WITH: a synthesized \"competitive whitespace\" — what NONE of them do well that a fused oracle-confirmed + cryptographically-provable + hard-governed + sovereign + Claude-powered autonomous agent could own. Cite URLs. Be thorough and specific — this defines the innovation target."},"uuid":"d4e79c6c-905e-42ea-8599-7d014177c52d","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:34:15.995Z","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

{"parentUuid":"4d00f9eb-2afe-4200-a555-a34758392213","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","attachment":{"type":"skill_listing","content":"- crucible: How to operate CRUCIBLE — the authorized, prove-don't-guess offensive-security engine in framework/v2. Read this BEFORE running an engagement/scan, re-verifying a finding, inspecting the event spine, reasoning about the veracity/anti-hallucination layer, the learning/metacognition core, or the safety stack. Use it when a task names CRUCIBLE, OBSIDIAN, `python3 -m framework.v2 <subcommand>`, findings/oracles/evidence certificates, the blackboard event spine, critics, reflection, or the reward/calibration loop.\n- deep-research: Deep research harness — fan-out web searches, fetch sources, adversarially verify claims, synthesize a cited report. - When the user wants a deep, multi-source, fact-checked research report on any topic. BEFORE invoking, check if the question is specific enough to research directly — if underspecified (e.g., \"what car to buy\" without budget/use-case/region), ask 2-3 clarifying questions to narrow scope. Then pass the refined question as args, weaving the answers in.\n- adversarial-sweep: Independent multi-lens adversarial re-attack of a defensive-oracle domain — the FORGE stage-9 companion to RED-PEN. Attack from disjoint lenses, refute each objection with a real repro, report only what survives. - Before merging any AEGIS/FORGE domain stream, alongside (not instead of) the red-pen agent. Pass the domain spec via args. On the email-auth stream it caught 3 CRITICAL/LOW defects PAST a RED-PEN PASS — near-zero-FP cannot be self-certified by one reviewer.\n- dataviz: Use this skill whenever you are about to create ANY chart, graph, plot, dashboard, or data visualization, in ANY output medium — an HTML or React artifact, inline SVG, plotting code in any library (matplotlib, plotly, d3, Recharts, …), an image/PNG you will render and upload, or a chart shared into Slack. Read it BEFORE writing the first line of chart code, choosing chart colors, building a stat tile / meter / KPI row, or laying out a dashboard. Produces visualizations that read as one system — elegant, accessible, consistent in light and dark — using a brand-neutral placeholder palette you swap for your own. Teaches a design-system-agnostic method: a form heuristic, a color formula with a runnable validator, mark specs, and interaction rules. A validated default palette is documented in `references/palette.md` — swap that file's values for your brand's. Triggers on: \"chart\", \"graph\", \"plot\", \"data viz\", \"visualization\", \"dashboard\", \"analytics\", \"visualize data\", \"categorical colors\", \"sequential / diverging palette\", \"stat tile\", \"sparkline\", \"heatmap\", \"legend\", \"axis\", \"tooltip\", \"chart colors\", \"color by series\".\n- artifact-design: Design guidance and fundamentals for Artifacts.\n- artifact-capabilities: Runtime capabilities a published Artifact page can be granted — behavior static HTML cannot provide on its own, such as the page reading live or connected data, keeping state shared across viewers, or updating and republishing itself. Serves this user's live capability roster and the typed call definitions. Load it whenever the user asks for an artifact needing any such runtime behavior.\n- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- verify: Verify that a code change actually does what it's supposed to by exercising it end-to-end and observing behavior — drive the affected flow, not just tests or typecheck. Run before committing nontrivial changes; bootstraps this repo's project verify skill if none exists yet. Don't invoke it on a diff that only touches tests, docs, or other code with no runtime surface to drive (a change to product source always has one) — there's nothing to observe.\n- code-review: Review the current diff for correctness bugs and reuse/simplification/efficiency cleanups at the given effort level (low/medium: fewer, high-confidence findings; high→max: broader coverage, may include uncertain findings; ultra: deep multi-agent review in the cloud). Pass --comment to post findings as inline PR comments, or --fix to apply the findings to the working tree after the review.\n- simplify: Review the changed code for reuse, simplification, efficiency, and altitude cleanups, then apply the fixes. Quality only — it does not hunt for bugs; use /code-review for that.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled cloud agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring cloud agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Reference for the Claude API / Anthropic SDK — model ids, pricing, params, streaming, tool use, MCP, agents, caching, token counting, model migration.\nTRIGGER — read BEFORE opening the target file; don't skip because it \"looks like a one-liner\" — whenever: the prompt names Claude/Anthropic in any form (Claude, Anthropic, Fable, Opus, Sonnet, Haiku, `anthropic`, `@anthropic-ai`, `claude-*`, `us.anthropic.*`, `[1m]`); the user asks about an LLM (pricing/model choice/limits/caching) — never answer from memory; OR the task is LLM-shaped with provider unstated (agent/MCP/tool-definition/multi-agent/RAG/LLM-judge/computer-use; generate/summarize/extract/classify/rewrite/converse over NL; debugging refusals/cutoffs/streaming/tool-calls/tokens).\nSKIP only when another provider is being worked on (overrides all triggers): OpenAI/GPT/Gemini/Llama/Mistral/Cohere/Ollama named in the query; OR `grep -rE 'openai|langchain_openai|google.generativeai|genai|mistralai|cohere|ollama'` over the project hits (run this grep FIRST if no provider named — don't Read the file).\n- run: Launch and drive this project's app to see a change working. Use when asked to run, start, or screenshot the app, or to confirm a change works in the real app (not just tests). First looks for a project skill that already covers launching the app; otherwise falls back to built-in patterns per project type (CLI, server, TUI, Electron, browser-driven, library).\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a GitHub pull request; for your working diff use /code-review\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch","skillCount":19,"isInitial":true,"names":["crucible","deep-research","adversarial-sweep","dataviz","artifact-design","artifact-capabilities","update-config","keybindings-help","verify","code-review","simplify","fewer-permission-prompts","loop","schedule","claude-api","run","init","review","security-review"]},"type":"attachment","uuid":"ae050ec9-6b55-4749-95c6-2e3b5047cd60","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:34:16.002Z","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Slashdot\",\"url\":\"https://it.slashdot.org/story/25/07/05/1847237/xbows-ai-powered-pentester-grabs-top-rank-on-hackerone-raises-75m-to-grow-platform\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's comprehensive information about XBOW's autonomous penetration testing and its HackerOne leaderboard achievement:\n\n## Overview\n\nXBOW's autonomous, AI-powered pen testing tool became the first non-human bug hunter to reach the top of HackerOne's US leaderboard in June. XBOW reached the #1 position on HackerOne's U.S. leaderboard in just 90 days, surpassing thousands of experienced bug bounty hunters and ethical hackers.\n\n## How It Works - Architecture\n\nThe AI doesn't just run pre-programmed scans. It actually thinks through problems—setting its own goals, writing custom code, debugging when things go wrong, and switching tactics based on what it finds.\n\nA critical component of XBOW's architecture is its validation approach. The trick to making a successful AI-driven pen tester was to use something other than an LLM to verify the vulnerabilities. In the case of XBOW, researchers used a deterministic validation approach. The company developed \"validators,\" — automated peer reviewers that confirm each uncovered vulnerability.\n\n## Performance and Results\n\nXBOW submitted nearly 1,060 vulnerabilities. All findings were fully automated, though our security team reviewed them pre-submission to comply with HackerOne's policy on automated tools.\n\nIn a live test across 104 real-world scenarios, a seasoned human pentester took 40 hours, whereas XBOW completed the same tasks in 28 minutes—an 85x speed advantage.\n\n## Limitations\n\nAround 25% of its findings are marked \"informative\" or \"not applicable.\" It shines at technical flaws like SQL injection, XSS, and remote code execution—but contextual issues, like patient privacy violations, still need human eyes.\n\nHuman oversight remains essential: Despite its autonomy, XBOW cannot submit reports without human review. This human oversight isn't optional—it's essential.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"0c936b3f-2f13-43df-aebf-d5915ef9732b","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:34.197Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"b0e18ce4-9ab7-4d37-a6b8-9ccd2adc113d","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

SecurityWeek\",\"url\":\"https://www.securityweek.com/autonomous-offensive-security-firm-xbow-raises-120m-at-1b-valuation/\"},{\"title\":\"AI Penetration Testing Platform | Continuous Offensive Security & Testing | XBOW\",\"url\":\"https://xbow.com/platform\"},{\"title\":\"How XBOW Ranked #1 in Autonomous Penetration Testing | XBOW\",\"url\":\"https://xbow.com/blog/top-1-how-xbow-did-it\"},{\"title\":\"Autonomous Offensive Security Firm XBOW Raises $35 Million - SecurityWeek\",\"url\":\"https://www.securityweek.com/autonomous-offensive-security-firm-xbow-raises-35-million/\"},{\"title\":\"Xbow Raises $120M to Let AI Agents Hack Your Systems Before Criminals Do | OpenClawAI\",\"url\":\"https://openclawai.io/blog/xbow-120m-autonomous-ai-hacking-pen-testing/\"},{\"title\":\"XBOW Benchmark: Autonomous Web Security Testing\",\"url\":\"https://www.emergentmind.com/topics/xbow-benchmark\"}]\n\nHere are the key findings from the search results about XBOW's autonomous pentesting validation, benchmark results, and funding:\n\n## Benchmark Performance\n\nXBOW matched the performance of a 20-year veteran pentester across 104 security challenges—completing in 28 minutes what took the human 40 hours. The XBOW Benchmark is a comprehensive set of 104 web security challenges that rigorously assess autonomous penetration testing frameworks using multi-agent systems, employing a multi-agent architecture—including coordinator, sandbox, and validation agents—to optimize exploit execution.\n\nAn autonomous cyber agent achieved 84.62% success rate on the XBOW benchmark, making it the leading open-source solution to reach XBOW baseline performance. It outperforms previous state-of-the-art systems like MAPTA (~75%) by nearly 10 percentage points and rivals expert human pentesters.\n\n## Real-World Validation\n\nIn June 2025, an AI system reached the number one spot on HackerOne's global leaderboard, outperforming thousands of human hackers. That system was XBOW. Over 90 days, the vulnerabilities submitted were classified as 54 critical, 242 high, 524 medium, and 65 low severity issues by program owners.\n\n## Funding\n\nXBOW raised a $75M Series B, led by Apoorv Agrawal of Altimeter, bringing total funding to $117M. More recently, XBOW raised $120M in Series C funding led by DFJ Growth and Northzone, bringing total funding to $237M and valuation above $1 billion.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"e097e5ea-cfff-4869-8091-68a0f91f6677","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:34.369Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"5bc9607c-dc5d-49b0-be4d-ab19a630b77c","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Project Zero\",\"url\":\"https://projectzero.google/2024/10/from-naptime-to-big-sleep.html\"},{\"title\":\"Project Zero, discovering vulnerabilities with LLM models\",\"url\":\"https://telefonicatech.com/en/blog/project-zero-discovering-vulnerabilities-with-llm\"},{\"title\":\"Google’s AI Tool Big Sleep Finds Zero-Day Vulnerability in SQLite Database Engine\",\"url\":\"https://thehackernews.com/2024/11/googles-ai-tool-big-sleep-finds-zero.html\"},{\"title\":\"Google researchers successfully found a zero-day vulnerability using LLM assisted vulnerability detection - SD Times\",\"url\":\"https://sdtimes.com/security/google-researchers-successfully-found-a-zero-day-vulnerability-using-llm-assisted-vulnerability-detection/\"},{\"title\":\"AI Cybersecurity in 2025: How Agents Are Finding Zero-Day Exploits | MindStudio\",\"url\":\"https://www.mindstudio.ai/blog/ai-cybersecurity-zero-day-exploits-2025\"},{\"title\":\"Google’s New AI Tool Uncovers Critical Zero-Day Vulnerability in SQLite - BigDATAwire\",\"url\":\"https://www.hpcwire.com/bigdatawire/2024/11/07/googles-new-ai-tool-uncovers-critical-zero-day-vulnerability-in-sqlite/\"},{\"title\":\"Cloud CISO Perspectives: Our Big Sleep agent makes a big leap | Google Cloud Blog\",\"url\":\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/identity-security/cloud-ciso-perspectives-our-big-sleep-agent-makes-big-leap\"},{\"title\":\"Google's Big Sleep: From Concept to Vulnerability Discovery | Cybersecurity Magazine\",\"url\":\"https://cybermagazine.com/articles/googles-big-sleep-from-concept-to-vulnerability-discovery\"},{\"title\":\"Google Claims World First As AI Finds 0-Day Security Vulnerability\",\"url\":\"https://www.forbes.com/sites/daveywinder/2024/11/05/google-claims-world-first-as-ai-finds-0-day-security-vulnerability/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's a comprehensive overview of the Google Project Zero Big Sleep vulnerability discovery results:\n\n## Overview\n\nProject Naptime has evolved into Big Sleep, a collaboration between Google Project Zero and Google DeepMind, which discovered the first real-world vulnerability using an LLM-assisted framework: an exploitable stack buffer underflow in SQLite, a widely used open source database engine.\n\n## Key Finding\n\nThis is the first public example of an AI agent finding a previously unknown exploitable memory-safety issue in widely used real-world software. The vulnerability was discovered and reported to the developers in early October, who fixed it the same day, and it was found before it appeared in an official release, so SQLite users were not impacted.\n\n## Project Background\n\nProject Naptime was introduced as part of research into offensive security capabilities of large language models. Big Sleep leverages an AI agent to simulate human behavior when identifying and demonstrating security vulnerabilities by taking advantage of an LLM's code comprehension and reasoning abilities, using specialized tools that allow the agent to navigate through the target codebase, run Python scripts in a sandboxed environment to generate inputs for fuzzing, and debug the program.\n\n## Current Assessment\n\nThe Big Sleep team emphasizes these are highly experimental results, and they believe that at present, a target-specific fuzzer would be at least as effective at finding vulnerabilities. However, they hope that in the future this effort will lead to a significant advantage to defenders, with the potential to provide high-quality root-cause analysis and make triaging and fixing issues much cheaper and more effective.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"344b56ac-a439-481d-b398-e896432c1a63","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:34.994Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"7fc5f7f7-2056-4cfd-ad45-89f1f901fc5a","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

The Trail of Bits Blog\",\"url\":\"https://blog.trailofbits.com/2025/08/09/trail-of-bits-buttercup-wins-2nd-place-in-aixcc-challenge/\"},{\"title\":\"AI Cyber Challenge\",\"url\":\"https://aicyberchallenge.com/\"},{\"title\":\"DARPA Announces Winners of AI Cyber Challenge – MeriTalk\",\"url\":\"https://www.meritalk.com/articles/darpa-announces-winners-of-ai-cyber-challenge/\"},{\"title\":\"Georgia Tech Makes History, Wins DARPA Challenge | School of Cybersecurity and Privacy\",\"url\":\"https://scp.cc.gatech.edu/external-news/georgia-tech-makes-history-wins-darpa-challenge\"},{\"title\":\"AIxCC: AI Cyber Challenge | Ep 89 | DARPA\",\"url\":\"https://www.darpa.mil/news/podcast/aixcc-challenge-89\"},{\"title\":\"DARPA Announces AI Cyber Challenge Winners\",\"url\":\"https://www.executivegov.com/articles/darpa-ai-cyber-challenge-open-source-software-team-atlanta-cyber-reasoning\"}]\n\nBased on the web search results, here's information about the DARPA AIxCC AI Cyber Challenge winners:\n\n## Winners\n\nTeam Atlanta's cyber reasoning system won the DARPA AI Cyber Challenge (AIxCC), a two-year, first-of-its-kind competition in collaboration with ARPA-H and frontier labs.\n\nTeam Atlanta comprises experts from Georgia Tech, Samsung Research, the Korea Advanced Institute of Science & Technology (KAIST), and the Pohang University of Science and Technology (POSTECH).\n\nSecond-place winner Trail of Bits is a New York City-based small business, and third-place winner Theori comprises AI researchers and security professionals in the U.S. and Korea. The teams received $4 million, $3 million, and $1.5 million, respectively, for their performance in the Final Competition.\n\n## Key Techniques and Results\n\nCompetitors successfully demonstrated the ability of novel autonomous systems using AI to secure the open-source software that underlies critical infrastructure.\n\nAIxCC was a two-year competition open to the public to see who could build the best fully automated system for securing open-source software. The scoring algorithm rewarded teams for finding vulnerabilities, proving that vulnerabilities existed, and correctly applying patches to open-source software. Speed and accuracy were also rewarded. Human interaction was strictly prohibited.\n\nIn total, competitors' systems discovered 54 unique synthetic vulnerabilities in the Final Competition's 63 challenges. Of those, they patched 43. In the Final Competition, teams also discovered 18 real, non-synthetic vulnerabilities that are being responsibly disclosed to open source project maintainers.\n\n## Regarding \"Atlantis\" and \"Buttercup\"\n\nI found information about Trail of Bits' system called \"Buttercup,\" but I did not find any team or system called \"Atlantis Trail of Bits\" in the search results. provoiceservices/strix-pentest: Open-source AI agents for penetration testing · GitHub\",\"url\":\"https://github.com/provoiceservices/strix-pentest\"},{\"title\":\"Strix: The Open-Source AI Pentester That Proves Every Bug (2026 Guide) | Rohit Raj\",\"url\":\"https://rohitraj.tech/ar/notes/strix-ai-penetration-testing-agent-guide-2026\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about Strix:\n\n## Overview\n\nStrix are autonomous AI penetration testing agents that act just like real hackers - they run your code dynamically, find vulnerabilities, and validate them through actual proofs-of-concept. Strix is an open-source, agentic security platform that helps teams identify exploitable vulnerabilities and security misconfigurations across modern apps, with AI agents that plan, investigate, and validate issues end-to-end.\n\n## Architecture\n\nStrix uses a multi-agent architecture where specialized AI agents collaborate to perform comprehensive security assessments, including Reconnaissance Agents to map attack surface, Exploitation Agents to attempt exploitation of vulnerabilities, Validation Agents to verify findings, and Post-Exploitation Agents to assess impact. The agents share discoveries in real-time, chain vulnerabilities together, and scale testing across multiple targets in parallel.\n\n## Capabilities\n\nStrix agents come equipped with a comprehensive offensive security toolkit including HTTP Interception Proxy, Browser Exploitation, Shell & Command Execution, Custom Exploit Runtime, Reconnaissance & OSINT, Static & Dynamic Code Analysis, and a Vulnerability Knowledge Base.\n\n## GitHub & Adoption\n\nStrix is a trending open-source AI penetration testing tool with 36,000+ GitHub stars. It hit #1 on GitHub Trending on July 3, 2026 with +2,137 stars in a day. The official repository is available at **https://github.com/usestrix/strix**.\n\n## Documentation\n\nFull documentation is available at docs.strix.ai - including detailed guides for usage, CI/CD integrations, skills, and advanced configuration.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"b85aef85-13f4-4040-8ec2-e777e2a21ba2","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:55.501Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"df0a7de2-7df9-4bbc-b152-cc7b28c76c05","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

NodeZero offers proof of every exploit, detailed remediation guidance, and 1-click verify to help you immediately confirm that your fixes are effective.\n\n## Real Attack Execution\n\nRather than theoretical findings, NodeZero shows you what can actually happen—today, in your environment, using real exploits. NodeZero pivots through your network, chaining together weaknesses just as an attacker would and then safely exploits them.\n\n## Exploit Validation at Scale\n\nNodeZero delivers autonomous pentesting at scale, executing dynamic attack paths that mimic real-world adversaries to identify exploitable weaknesses. In 2024, NodeZero exploited 229 known vulnerabilities (CVEs) a staggering 99,924 times within customer environments, demonstrating the scale of the slow patching dilemma.\n\n## Verification of Fixes\n\nClose the loop on every fix. Don't just check a box. Re-test to confirm the threat is gone and prove your risk has actually been reduced.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"d9e00340-08b2-42fd-8912-fb6fcde7f783","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:55.690Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9bd51022-1b84-4c7a-980e-dedb862782ce","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Hours\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/html/2605.04019v1\"},{\"title\":\"PyRIT AI Red Teaming: Metasploit for LLMs\",\"url\":\"https://www.toxsec.com/p/pyrit-ai-red-teaming\"},{\"title\":\"Redefining AI Medium\",\"url\":\"https://medium.com/@adnanmasood/red-teaming-generative-ai-managing-operational-risk-ff1862931844\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about these AI red teaming frameworks:\n\n## Overview of Major Frameworks\n\nThe most widely adopted AI red teaming frameworks include Microsoft's PyRIT, NVIDIA's Garak, Promptfoo, and Meta's Purple Llama CyberSecEval.\n\n## PyRIT (Microsoft)\n\nPyRIT (Python Risk Identification Toolkit) is a comprehensive framework developed by Microsoft AI Red Team for probing for security and safety vulnerabilities in generative AI systems. PyRIT enables multi-turn attack strategies like Crescendo, TAP, and Skeleton Key across different modalities, supporting text-to-text, audio, image, video, and file conversions. The framework provides scoring subsystems with true/false, Likert scale, classification, and custom scorers powered by LLMs, Azure AI Content Safety, or custom logic, enabling quantitative evaluation across diverse AI deployments including OpenAI, Azure, Anthropic, Google, HuggingFace, and custom endpoints.\n\n## Garak (NVIDIA)\n\nNVIDIA's Garak focuses on LLM vulnerability scanning with an extensive probe library. Garak is NVIDIA's broad-spectrum vulnerability scanner, closer to Nmap for LLMs.\n\n## Meta's CyberSecEval\n\nMeta's Purple Llama CyberSecEval is among the most widely adopted frameworks, and it serves as a measurement standard alongside the OWASP LLM Top 10 for regulatory documentation.\n\n## Comparative Use Cases\n\nPyRIT is the deep, adaptive multi-turn attack engine, Garak sweeps the surface, PyRIT runs the surgical follow-up, Promptfoo keeps patches from regressing.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"186ca77c-235f-4188-9656-4a58e0d7c587","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:56.696Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"4fdd45b1-66ad-4ba8-9d23-3ef9776b19f5","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}
{"parentUuid":"147dffae-6b63-4496-ae33-89d073aebaba","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"7ce8881e-5fa8-4b31-a62a-9ff4d72be4e8","agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01R7L9gF2yEy4CevD8GKzvEe","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"CAI Cybersecurity AI framework HackingBuddyGPT PentestGPT open source pentest agent architecture\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Top 10 Open-Source AI Agent Penetration Testing Projects\",\"url\":\"https://blog.spark42.tech/top-10-open-source-ai-agent-penetration-testing-projects/\"},{\"title\":\"AI Pentesting Agents 2026: The Rise of 39+ Tools Tested\",\"url\":\"https://appsecsanta.com/research/ai-pentesting-agents-2026\"},{\"title\":\"GitHub - aliasrobotics/cai: Cybersecurity AI (CAI), the framework for AI Security · GitHub\",\"url\":\"https://github.com/aliasrobotics/CAI\"},{\"title\":\"Cybersecurity AI ( CAI\",\"url\":\"https://aliasrobotics.github.io/cai/\"},{\"title\":\"GitHub - GreyDGL/PentestGPT: Automated Penetration Testing Agentic Framework Powered by Large Language Models · GitHub\",\"url\":\"https://github.com/GreyDGL/PentestGPT\"},{\"title\":\"8 Open-Source AI Pentest Tools for Security Teams in 2026 | Ostorlab: Mobile App Security Testing for Android and iOS\",\"url\":\"https://blog.ostorlab.co/8-open-source-ai-pentest-tools-2026.html\"},{\"title\":\"Cybersecurity AI Beyond CTFs, What CAI Means for Real Pentesting\",\"url\":\"https://www.penligent.ai/hackinglabs/cybersecurity-ai-beyond-ctfs-what-cai-means-for-real-pentesting/\"},{\"title\":\"CAI, the Future of AI in Cybersecurity – Deep Hacking\",\"url\":\"https://blog.deephacking.tech/en/posts/cai-ai-cybersecurity/\"},{\"title\":\"CAI: The framework revolutionizing cybersecurity AI testing | Alias Robotics News\",\"url\":\"https://news.aliasrobotics.com/cai-the-framework-revolutionizing-cybersecurity-ai-testing/\"},{\"title\":\"The AI Hacking Boom: What 70 New Offensive Security Tools Mean for Defenders\",\"url\":\"https://hadrian.io/blog/the-ai-offensive-security-boom-seventy-tools-in-eighteen-months\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's comprehensive information about the CAI Cybersecurity AI framework and related projects:\n\n## CAI (Cybersecurity AI) Framework\n\nCybersecurity AI (CAI) is an open-source framework that evolved from PentestGPT, created by the original PentestGPT authors to be a more robust, extensible platform. It's a lightweight, ergonomic framework for building bug bounty-ready Cybersecurity AIs.\n\n### Key Capabilities\n\nCAI is a lightweight extensible framework supporting 300+ model backends with built-in tools for reconnaissance, exploitation, and privilege escalation, plus self-hosted LLM support for air-gapped environments.\n\nIt's positioned as a \"bug bounty–ready\" AI assistant for offensive (and defensive) security, with multi-agent orchestration, extensive tooling, and support for local models.\n\n### Agent Architecture\n\nThe architecture is based on agents (small focused AIs for web recon, binary exploitation, etc.), tools that integrate with existing security tools like Nmap, Gobuster, Frida, Hashcat, Burp, and Ghidra, and patterns to coordinate agents including a Red Team Agent for pentesting and a Bug Bounty Hunter for vulnerability hunting.\n\n## Related Projects\n\n### PentestGPT\n\nPentestGPT is the pioneer with three self-interacting modules: Reasoning, Generation, Parsing, each maintaining its own LLM session to manage context. PentestGPT requires a powerful model (ideally GPT-4 via API) and remains a prototype (the authors recommend using CAI now).\n\n### HackingBuddyGPT\n\nHackingBuddyGPT executes shell commands inside sandboxes, reads the output, and decides what to do next. It shows how minimal it can get — about 50 lines of Python, no framework, no database, no middleware, connecting over SSH, sending commands, and feeding output back.\n\n### Real-World Performance\n\nCAI ran a structured comparison against expert human testers and found a 156-times cost reduction, with a representative engagement costing $109 versus $17,218 for the human-led equivalent, while running 3,600 times faster.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"8860516d-bced-41bb-a2cf-cc698d128a96","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:35:59.732Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"147dffae-6b63-4496-ae33-89d073aebaba","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Overview\nTeam Atlanta's Atlantis is an AI-powered cyber reasoning system that combines cutting-edge LLM technology with advanced vulnerability analysis to create the foundation for Security-AGI. Team Atlanta won 1st place in DARPA's AI Cyber Challenge (AIxCC).\n\n## System Architecture\nATLANTIS integrates large language models (LLMs) with program analysis by combining symbolic execution, directed fuzzing, and static analysis to address limitations in automated vulnerability discovery and program repair.\n\nAtlantis is built around an ensemble philosophy where any technique demonstrating unique contribution is incorporated and combined.\n\n## Competition Context\nAIxCC (2023-2025) challenged teams to build autonomous cyber reasoning systems capable of discovering and patching vulnerabilities at the speed and scale of modern software. Atlantis is a fully autonomous system that when given a project's source code will detect and fix bugs within a time-constrained setting.\n\n## Team\nThe distinguished Team Atlanta brings together expertise from Georgia Tech, Samsung Research, KAIST, and POSTECH, with proven success at Pwn2Own, DEFCON CTF, and leading academic venues.\n\nThe system represents a significant advancement in using LLMs for autonomous cybersecurity tasks by integrating multiple analysis techniques.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"930e45c9-651b-4f76-abb0-234362ecb249","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:36:30.439Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"3363e424-3909-411b-986d-9e25f343e7a6","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

It runs 20+ security tools in an isolated Docker sandbox with memory and web intelligence.\n\n**Architecture:** An orchestrator agent receives a goal and coordinates three specialist agents: a researcher that gathers information and queries known vulnerability sources, a developer that plans attack strategies, and an executor that runs commands in isolated containers. Each agent type draws on three memory layers: long-term vector storage, working context, and episodic history, all backed by PostgreSQL with the pgvector extension for semantic search.\n\n**Key Features:**\n- The platform operates 'with no human in the loop' once tasks are defined, automatically determining and executing penetration testing steps without manual intervention.\n- For security work, it defaults to a Kali Linux image pre-loaded with more than 20 tools including nmap, Metasploit, and sqlmap.\n- Graphiti-powered knowledge graph using Neo4j for semantic relationship tracking and advanced context understanding.\n\n**LLM Support:** PentAGI accepts credentials for OpenAI, Anthropic, Google Gemini, AWS Bedrock, Ollama, DeepSeek, OpenRouter, and several others.\n\n## Nebula\n\nIt's important to note that Nebula is an interactive AI terminal assistant, not an autonomous penetration testing tool, and is used to aid with human-driven testing by assisting with commands, payloads, and analysis rather than producing standalone findings.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"1a3bc766-2fed-4f0e-a3be-c1e103560225","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:36:32.504Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"890aea89-1e8b-48eb-bc91-6d2ae435f17f","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

- The Trail of Bits Blog\",\"url\":\"https://blog.trailofbits.com/2025/08/08/buttercup-is-now-open-source/\"},{\"title\":\"AIxCC finals: Tale of the tape - The Trail of Bits Blog\",\"url\":\"https://blog.trailofbits.com/2025/08/07/aixcc-finals-tale-of-the-tape/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's information about Trail of Bits' Buttercup AIxCC system:\n\n## Overview\n\nButtercup is a Cyber Reasoning System (CRS) that combines conventional cybersecurity techniques like fuzzing and static analysis with AI and machine learning to find and fix software vulnerabilities. Buttercup won the $3M second prize at DARPA's AIxCC.\n\n## Architecture\n\nA multi-agent architecture runs the whole pipeline without a human in the loop. The system has several key components:\n\n- **Orchestration/UI**: Orchestration/UI coordinates the overall actions of Buttercup's other components and displays information about vulnerabilities discovered and patches generated by the system, and also reports logs and system events to a SigNoz telemetry server.\n\n- **Vulnerability Discovery**: Uses AI-augmented mutational fuzzing to find program inputs that demonstrate vulnerabilities.\n\n- **Patching System**: Uses coordinated AI agents to analyze vulnerabilities and generate patches, including specialized agents for root cause analysis, code context retrieval, and software engineering tasks.\n\n- **Program Analysis**: Integrates static analysis tools like tree-sitter and code query systems and understands call graphs, dependencies, and vulnerability contexts.\n\n## How It Works\n\nThe system uses large language models (LLMs) to generate seed inputs for fuzzing, significantly reducing the time needed to discover vulnerabilities. More specifically, it augments both libFuzzer and Jazzer with LLM-generated test cases.\n\nButtercup discovers and patches real vulnerabilities using static analysis and AI-guided fuzzing, then proves each bug and verifies each fix before reporting it, which kept Buttercup's results at 90% accuracy.\n\nNotably, Trail of Bits achieved these results using exclusively less expensive, non-reasoning LLMs.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"8a1f161c-52d8-45e4-a87d-27623dab63fd","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:36:33.122Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"a187fcd6-f09f-414d-bf2a-9a41abd28463","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Sleep's 2025 vulnerability discoveries:\n\n## Real-World Vulnerabilities Found by Big Sleep\n\nBy November 2024, Big Sleep was able to find its first real-world security vulnerability, and since then, Big Sleep has continued to discover multiple real-world vulnerabilities, exceeding expectations.\n\nThe specific CVEs discovered by Big Sleep in 2025 include:\n\n1. **CVE-2025-6965** (SQLite): The Big Sleep agent discovered an SQLite vulnerability (CVE-2025-6965) — a critical security flaw, and one that was known only to threat actors and was at risk of being exploited. This was particularly significant as this is the first time an AI agent has been used to directly foil efforts to exploit a vulnerability in the wild.\n\n2. **CVE-2025-9478** (Chrome ANGLE): The flaw, tracked as CVE-2025-9478, was discovered on August 11, 2025, not by a human researcher—but by Big Sleep, Google's AI-powered vulnerability discovery agent.\n\n3. **CVE-2025-9132** (Chrome V8): The vulnerability is an out-of-bounds write issue in the V8 JavaScript engine that was discovered by Big Sleep AI.\n\n4. **Earlier SQLite flaw**: In October 2024, Big Sleep was behind the discovery of another flaw in SQLite, a stack buffer underflow vulnerability that could have been exploited to result in a crash or arbitrary code execution.\n\nBig Sleep was developed by Google DeepMind in collaboration with Project Zero.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"e51bedf5-46ce-46dc-b547-7a2fbd3e354f","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:37:07.113Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"bc99b770-c11d-48c1-8304-22278d3ecfd4","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

{"parentUuid":"f20ef7ba-3585-44a3-b6e3-1b6c833dd358","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"7ce8881e-5fa8-4b31-a62a-9ff4d72be4e8","agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01CLPAuFa6vxS1pCncFwzL4N","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"XBOW false positives criticism open source maintainers AI slop bug bounty noise limitations\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"AI-SLOP: Develop best current practises for Open Source maintainers · Issue #178 · ossf/wg-vulnerability-disclosures\",\"url\":\"https://github.com/ossf/wg-vulnerability-disclosures/issues/178\"},{\"title\":\"HeroDevs Blog | How AI Broke Open Source Security: End-of-Life Software Is the Most Exposed\",\"url\":\"https://www.herodevs.com/blog-posts/how-ai-broke-open-source-security-end-of-life-most-exposed\"},{\"title\":\"AI Security Tool XBOW Tops US Bug Bounty Rankings But Raises Concerns About Automated Vulnerability Flooding - BigGo News\",\"url\":\"https://biggo.com/news/202506250113_AI_Tool_XBOW_Tops_Bug_Bounty_Rankings\"},{\"title\":\"Top XBOW Alternatives in 2026: Escape vs XBOW & 4 More Tools\",\"url\":\"https://escape.tech/blog/xbow-alternatives/\"},{\"title\":\"XBOW, an autonomous penetration tester, has reached the top spot on HackerOne | Hacker News\",\"url\":\"https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=44367548\"},{\"title\":\"AI-powered bug hunting shakes up bounty industry — for better or worse | CSO Online\",\"url\":\"https://www.csoonline.com/article/4082265/ai-powered-bug-hunting-shakes-up-bounty-industry-for-better-or-worse.html\"},{\"title\":\"How XBOW Ranked #1 in Autonomous Penetration Testing | XBOW\",\"url\":\"https://xbow.com/blog/top-1-how-xbow-did-it\"},{\"title\":\"AI slop and fake reports are coming for your bug bounty programs | TechCrunch\",\"url\":\"https://techcrunch.com/2025/07/24/ai-slop-and-fake-reports-are-exhausting-some-security-bug-bounties/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about XBOW, false positives, and criticisms related to open source maintainers, AI-generated reports (\"AI slop\"), and bug bounty programs:\n\n## XBOW Overview\n\nXBOW, an autonomous penetration testing tool, reached number one on HackerOne's US rankings by discovering over 1,000 vulnerabilities across various companies and programs. XBOW hit #1 on HackerOne's US leaderboard in June 2025 with 1,060 submitted vulnerabilities.\n\n## False Positives and Validation\n\nAutomation has long struggled with false positives, and nowhere is this more evident than in vulnerability scanning. Tools that flag dozens of irrelevant issues often create more work than they save. However, the company behind XBOW built sophisticated validation systems to avoid the false positive problem that plagues many automated security tools, using what they call validators - automated peer reviewers that confirm each vulnerability.\n\n## Broader AI Slop Crisis\n\nDespite XBOW's validation efforts, the wider ecosystem faces significant challenges from AI-generated reports:\n\n- Projects are receiving high volumes of low-quality vulnerability reports that appear to be generated by AI with minimal or no human review, creating a \"DDoS-like situation\" for maintainers.\n\n- Halfway through 2025, curl reported that only ~5% of bug bounty submissions were genuine vulnerabilities, with around 20% appearing to be AI-generated slop.\n\n- Some projects have been forced to discontinue bug bounty programs entirely (e.g., curl ended their bug bounty program in January 2026), while others like Node.js have implemented stricter signal requirements on HackerOne.\n\n- HackerOne has seen a rise in false positives — vulnerabilities that appear real but are generated by LLMs and lack real-world impact.\n\n## Maintainer Impact\n\nValidating these reports can take significant time, consuming valuable volunteer time and resources, and the constant stream of low-quality reports contributes to stress, frustration, and burnout - especially for unpaid volunteer maintainers.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"14dbcede-eb0e-46da-ac5c-d823bd3ac036","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:37:40.207Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"f20ef7ba-3585-44a3-b6e3-1b6c833dd358","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

DARPA's AI Cyber Challenge (AIxCC): Competition Design, Architectures, and Lessons Learned\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07666\"},{\"title\":\"Seungjoo Kim (김승주) on X: \\\"SoK: DARPA's AI Cyber Challenge (AIxCC): Competition Design, Architectures, and Lessons Learned https://t.co/NDCXlf3UKr\\\" / X\",\"url\":\"https://x.com/skim71/status/2021324646382248310\"},{\"title\":\"Code-Augur: Security\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/401719284_OSS-CRS_Liberating_AIxCC_Cyber_Reasoning_Systems_for_Real-World_Open-Source_Security\"},{\"title\":\"Direction for Detection: A Survey of Automated Vulnerability Detection and all of its Pain Points\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.11194\"}]\n\nI found comprehensive information about the AIxCC SoK paper. Here's what the search revealed:\n\n## About the Paper\n\nDARPA's AI Cyber Challenge (AIxCC, 2023–2025) is the largest competition to date for building fully autonomous Cyber Reasoning Systems (CRSs) that leverage recent advances in AI—particularly large language models (LLMs)—to discover and remediate vulnerabilities in real-world open-source software.\n\n## Paper Content\n\nThe paper examines the competition's structure and key design decisions, characterizes the architectural approaches of finalist CRSs, and analyzes competition results beyond the final scoreboard.\n\nThe analysis reveals the factors that truly drove CRS performance, identifies genuine technical advances achieved by teams, and exposes limitations that remain open for future research, concluding with lessons for organizing future competitions and broader insights toward deploying autonomous CRSs in practice.\n\n## Competition Scale\n\nThe final competition in August 2025 represents the largest-scale evaluation of autonomous vulnerability analysis to date: around 143 hours of fully autonomous operation, CRSs from seven finalist teams analyzed 53 challenge projects derived from critical infrastructure software, each equipped with $85K in cloud compute and $50K in LLM API credits.\n\n## Competition Tasks\n\nTeams developed Cyber Reasoning Systems that operated on version-controlled repositories containing full source code in C/C++ or Java of real-world open-source projects, with vulnerabilities synthetically introduced into the commit history, requiring autonomous detection, exploitation, and patching.\n\nThe paper appears to be published by researchers from Georgia Institute of Technology, Texas A&M University, and other institutions, and is available on arXiv responsible scaling policies, and Cybench benchmark performance:\n\n## Cybench Benchmark Overview\n\nCybench is a public benchmark using CTF challenges to evaluate LLMs, and Anthropic has featured Cybench in multiple Claude model system cards, including Claude 3.7 Sonnet, Claude 4, Claude Opus 4.1, Claude Sonnet 4.5, and Claude Mythos Preview.\n\n## Progressive Performance Improvements\n\nClaude 3.7 Sonnet solves about a third of the Cybench challenges within five attempts, up from about five percent with Anthropic's frontier model from the previous year. More recently, Claude Sonnet 4.5 succeeds on 76.5% of the challenges when given 10 attempts at the Cybench evaluation.\n\n## Claude Mythos Preview Saturation\n\nClaude Mythos Preview has saturated Anthropic's CTF-style evaluations, achieving 100% pass@1 on Cybench across all 35 tested challenges. However, Anthropic chose not to release it because the cyber capability profile was too severe, though this was described as a judgment call rather than a requirement of Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy.\n\n## Benchmark Limitations\n\nAnthropic states the Cybench benchmark is \"no longer sufficiently informative of current frontier model capabilities\", leading to the development of newer evaluations like CyberGym with larger sample sizes to better assess frontier model differences.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ea7d0585-f998-4fd9-8517-a18c524c5e12","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:03.710Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"e0403f8c-4f24-47d4-8cb0-8e54523e17b0","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Michael Bargury\",\"url\":\"https://www.mbgsec.com/archive/2025-07-20-project-zero-project-naptime-evaluating-offensive-security-capabilities-of-large-language-models/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's information about Project Naptime:\n\n## Overview\n\nNaptime uses a specialised architecture to enhance an LLM's ability to perform vulnerability research. The objective of the Naptime framework is to enable an LLM to perform vulnerability research that closely mimics human security experts' iterative, hypothesis-driven approach.\n\n## Architecture and Tools\n\nThe Naptime architecture is centred around the interaction between an AI agent and a target codebase. The tools include a code browser, a Python interpreter, a debugger, and a reporting tool, which allow the agent to navigate the code base, execute Python scripts, interact with the program and observe its behavior, and communicate its progress in a structured way.\n\nSpecifically:\n- The Code Browser tool enables the agent to navigate through the target codebase, much like how engineers use Chromium Code Search.\n- The Python enables the agent to run Python scripts in a sandboxed environment for intermediate calculations and to generate precise and complex inputs to the target program.\n- The Debugger allows the agent to interact with the program and observe its behavior under different inputs.\n\n## Key Features\n\nThe system is model-agnostic and backend-agnostic, providing a self-contained vulnerability research environment. The framework increased CyberSecEval2 benchmark performance by up to 20x from the original paper.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"b678aa19-92d7-44b7-a8d2-4115f91823e3","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:04.340Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"e07fafed-4d23-44d1-9383-0e48eae2aa59","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

they run your code dynamically, find vulnerabilities, and validate them through actual proofs-of-concept. Strix is an open-source autonomous AI penetration testing framework that deploys multi-agent hackers to discover, validate, and prove vulnerabilities through real proof-of-concepts.\n\n## Sandbox & Proof-of-Concept Validation\n\nA key distinguishing feature is the PoC requirement. The rule that makes it interesting: it won't report a vulnerability until it has actually exploited it. Every finding ships with a reproducible proof-of-concept. No PoC, no finding. Strix's answer is structural: the proof-of-concept requirement is the hallucination filter. An agent can imagine an SQL injection, but it can't produce a working PoC exploit for a bug that isn't there. If the exploit doesn't fire, the finding doesn't ship.\n\n## Self-Hosted & Local LLM Support\n\nWith a local LLM (Ollama, LMStudio) and the self-hosted Docker sandbox, Strix can run fully air-gapped. No data leaves your environment. Strix supports multiple LLM providers including OpenAI GPT-5.4, Anthropic Claude Sonnet 4.6, Google Gemini 3 Pro, and local models via Ollama/LMStudio.\n\n## Strix vs XBOW Positioning\n\nThis is the builder's read — what it is, how to install and run it, whether it hallucinates, how it stacks up against XBOW and PentAGI. Independent research cataloguing 39 of these tools in 2026 puts Strix among the most reliable open-source options, alongside commercial agents like XBOW.\n\n## Performance Benchmark\n\nOn the XBEN benchmark — 104 real-world web security challenges in CTF format — a tested build of Strix solved 100 of 104 (96%), averaging ~19 minutes per challenge at a total cost of $337, about $3.37 per challenge.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"82564c56-95ac-45d5-bdbf-147b0e4d6302","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:07.136Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"f281070d-01b5-4aa5-bc51-568681ffed79","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

{"parentUuid":"ff7343b0-c37e-4ceb-99ec-42d2525c8f82","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"7ce8881e-5fa8-4b31-a62a-9ff4d72be4e8","agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_015Mz2vi8muRgyiyLB6tA3gA","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Atlantis AIxCC ensemble multilang multiple CRS proof of vulnerability PoV patch verification results bugs found finals\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Overview | Team-Atlanta/aixcc-afc-atlantis\",\"url\":\"https://zread.ai/Team-Atlanta/aixcc-afc-atlantis\"},{\"title\":\"Team Atlanta +\",\"url\":\"https://taesoo.kim/pubs/2025/kim:atlantis.pdf\"},{\"title\":\"ATLANTIS\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.14589\"},{\"title\":\"AIxCC Final and Team Atlanta\",\"url\":\"https://team-atlanta.github.io/blog/post-afc/\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) ATLANTIS: AI-driven Threat Localization, Analysis, and Triage Intelligence System\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/395649167_ATLANTIS_AI-driven_Threat_Localization_Analysis_and_Triage_Intelligence_System\"},{\"title\":\"[Literature Review] ATLANTIS: AI-driven Threat Localization, Analysis, and Triage Intelligence System\",\"url\":\"https://www.themoonlight.io/en/review/atlantis-ai-driven-threat-localization-analysis-and-triage-intelligence-system\"},{\"title\":\"Atlantis Infrastructure\",\"url\":\"https://team-atlanta.github.io/blog/post-atl-infra/\"},{\"title\":\"OSS-CRS: Liberating AIxCC Cyber Reasoning Systems for Real-World Open-Source Security\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/html/2603.08566\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about Atlantis AIxCC and its vulnerability discovery results:\n\n## Overview\n\nAtlantis is a sophisticated, multi-component system engineered to automatically discover, analyze, and patch vulnerabilities across diverse software ecosystems. The primary goal of the AIxCC competition is to build a cyber reasoning system (CRS) that employs large language models (LLMs) to automatically find proof-of-vulnerabilities (PoVs) in challenge projects (CPs), fix the vulnerabilities, and assess given SARIF reports.\n\n## Architecture and Ensemble Approach\n\nEach CP-MANAGER is responsible for building the CP, allocating Azure and LLM budgets, and launching bug-finding modules (ATLANTIS-C for C/C++, ATLANTIS-Java for Java, and ATLANTIS-Multilang for cross-language fuzzing), along with ATLANTIS-Patch for patch generation and ATLANTIS-SARIF for SARIF report assessment.\n\n## Patch Verification\n\nFor patching, the Proof-of-Vulnerability (PoV) serves as our oracle. We validate patches by re-running the PoV against patched programs.\n\n## Final Competition Results\n\nDuring the final, all competing CRSs collectively discovered 6 C/C++ bugs and 12 Java bugs in real-world software. Atlantis contributed 3 of each category, including a 0-day vulnerability in SQLite discovered during the semi-final.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"a6bf00bb-286f-4bd3-839c-912a7ccdf428","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:33.586Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"ff7343b0-c37e-4ceb-99ec-42d2525c8f82","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}
{"parentUuid":"65313124-2b85-44e3-93b5-787ad6e1d804","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"7ce8881e-5fa8-4b31-a62a-9ff4d72be4e8","agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01MVvwFiavWWhcGF25iz2MvR","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"autonomous penetration testing scope enforcement rules of engagement guardrails authorization safety controls\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"XBOW - Ethical Considerations in AI-Driven Penetration Testing: A Governance Framework for Security Teams\",\"url\":\"https://xbow.com/blog/ethical-considerations-ai-pentesting\"},{\"title\":\"Glossary\",\"url\":\"https://owasp.org/APTS/standard/appendix/Glossary.html\"},{\"title\":\"What are the Rules of Engagement in Penetration Testing? | ioSENTRIX\",\"url\":\"https://iosentrix.com/blog/rules-of-engagement-in-penetration-testing\"},{\"title\":\"Rules of Engagement Penetration Testing\",\"url\":\"https://www.fortifyframework.com/rules-of-engagement-penetration-testing/\"},{\"title\":\"Penetration Testing Rules of Engagement: Scope, ROE ...\",\"url\":\"https://blog.securelayer7.net/penetration-testing-rules-of-engagement/\"},{\"title\":\"Scope Enforcement - APTS-SE\",\"url\":\"https://owasp.org/APTS/standard/1_Scope_Enforcement/\"},{\"title\":\"Rules of Engagement in Penetration Testing | Penetration Testing Authority\",\"url\":\"https://penetrationtestingauthority.com/rules-of-engagement-penetration-testing/\"},{\"title\":\"APTS/standard/Introduction.md at main · OWASP/APTS\",\"url\":\"https://github.com/OWASP/APTS/blob/main/standard/Introduction.md\"}]\n\nThe search returned comprehensive information on autonomous penetration testing scope enforcement, rules of engagement, guardrails, authorization, and safety controls. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Rules of Engagement (RoE)\n\nA Rules of Engagement is a formal document defining the scope, boundaries, authorized activities, temporal constraints, escalation procedures, and contact information for an autonomous penetration testing engagement. The RoE is the authoritative source for scope enforcement.\n\n## Scope Definition\n\nScope should define approved targets, environments, test accounts, user roles, production-versus-staging rules, testing windows, rate limits, third-party exclusions, and prohibited actions, such as destructive testing or data modification.\n\n## Authorization and Safety Controls\n\nAI-driven pentesting introduces new governance challenges around authorization, accountability, privacy, and explainability, requiring security teams to pair autonomous testing with enforceable controls, validated findings, and human oversight. Ethical AI penetration testing starts with explicit authorization, defined scope, and safe testing limits.\n\n## Enforcement Mechanisms\n\nSecurity teams should ask vendors how they enforce scope, what guardrails are built in, and whether testing can be paused or constrained. The system MUST enforce temporal boundaries (start time, end time) with sufficient precision for unambiguous enforcement (second or better) and MUST account for timezone conversions and daylight saving time changes.\n\n## Governance Framework\n\nThe OWASP Autonomous Penetration Testing Standard (APTS) defines governance requirements for autonomous pentesting platforms to operate safely, transparently, and within defined boundaries, addressing challenges unique to autonomous operation: scope enforcement, safe autonomy, manipulation resistance, and accountability.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ed621e37-d044-47dc-a289-21f43b26d263","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:33.903Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"65313124-2b85-44e3-93b5-787ad6e1d804","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

VentureBeat\",\"url\":\"https://venturebeat.com/security/openais-red-team-plan-make-chatgpt-agent-an-ai-fortress\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about OpenAI's preparedness framework, cyber capabilities, red teaming efforts, and Aardvark:\n\n## Aardvark - Autonomous Vulnerability Agent\n\nAardvark is an AI-powered security researcher that autonomously finds, validates, and helps fix software vulnerabilities at scale. It scans codebases for vulnerabilities and proposes patches that maintainers can adopt quickly. It has already identified novel CVEs in open-source software by reasoning over entire codebases.\n\n## Preparedness Framework\n\nOpenAI operates under a formal Safety and Preparedness Framework, which defines risk thresholds and evaluation criteria across misuse categories such as cybersecurity, CBRN, persuasion, and autonomy. The framework assessed models with a \"medium\" risk post-mitigation across all four domains—cybersecurity, CBRN, persuasion, and autonomy.\n\n## Red Teaming Approach\n\nExternal red teaming is a key part of this framework. Prior to deployment, models undergo adversarial testing by vetted external experts to identify alignment failures, injection vectors, tool misuse paths, and safety regressions. For GPT-5, OpenAI coordinated external capability assessments across key risk areas such as long horizon autonomy, scheming, deception and oversight subversion, wet lab planning feasibility, and offensive cybersecurity evaluations.\n\n## Cyber Capabilities\n\nGPT-5 scored 27% on a capture the flag challenge, but just a few months later GPT-5.1-Codex-Max scored 76%. This demonstrates rapid advancement in AI cyber capabilities that the company is actively monitoring through its preparedness framework.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"d8c79dfd-bb4e-4c1b-97c6-0c9216e3c48e","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:36.300Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"d8218b9f-9b27-4c4e-8d84-79cc9bdbdd19","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

SiliconANGLE\",\"url\":\"https://siliconangle.com/2026/05/20/terra-security-unifies-web-ai-network-testing-one-agentic-platform/\"},{\"title\":\"Terra Security Redefines Penetration Testing for the AI Era with Terra Portal\",\"url\":\"https://finance.yahoo.com/news/terra-security-redefines-penetration-testing-110000238.html\"},{\"title\":\"External Network Pentesting with Terra Platform™\",\"url\":\"https://www.terra.security/blog/external-network-pentesting-with-terra-platform\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's a comprehensive overview of how Terra Security's agentic pentesting with human-in-the-loop validation and contextual methodology works:\n\n## Overview\n\nTerra Platform unifies continuous agentic pentesting across AI systems, external networks, and web apps, with a Human-on-the-Loop to ensure production safety and compliance.\n\n## Dual Agent Model\n\nAt the core of Terra Portal is a human-governed, agentic workflow featuring two distinct types of AI agents. Ambient AI agents autonomously handle recon, code review, test case generation, reachability analysis, pentests, exploitability validation, documentation, and remediation. When complexity, risk, or organizational guardrails require expert judgment, pentesters engage with Copilot AI agents to conduct approved, controlled exploitation and reporting.\n\n## Contextual Understanding\n\nAgents are trained on your business context and code, so they can surface the threats that actually impact your business. Terra's severity scale is based on YOUR business context, providing proof of exploitability, probability, comparable breaches and potential business impact with an auto-remediation suggestion.\n\n## Continuous Validation Methodology\n\nTerra's platform runs continuously, triggered by changes across your web apps, APIs, cloud infrastructure, and network. When a new feature goes live, the AI agents immediately test it. When a configuration changes, the system re-evaluates exposure.\n\n## Human Oversight at Critical Points\n\nThe human expert sits at the controls, approving intrusive attacks, guiding the agents through complex multi-step exploits, and, when needed, providing the creativity that only humans currently possess. This means that when Terra flags a critical vulnerability, it has already been verified. No false positives.\n\n## Attack Surface Coverage\n\nTerra Platform covers all three foundational layers: web and internal applications, AI systems, and network infrastructure, with the same agentic AI platform and Human-in-the-Loop model across all of them.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"12d61710-f710-4962-ba3a-66b1889160d2","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:38:38.399Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"a1cd5ab6-910c-4033-b583-1f127c533820","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

Here's What the Industry Gets Wrong.\",\"url\":\"https://xbow.com/blog/we-ran-1060-autonomous-attacks\"},{\"title\":\"XBOW - Agentic Pentesting with ZERO False Positives | Aidan John\",\"url\":\"https://blog.aidanjohn.org/2025/09/27/xbow-agentic-pentesting-with-zero.html\"},{\"title\":\"AI Penetration Testing Platform | Continuous Offensive Security & Testing | XBOW\",\"url\":\"https://xbow.com/platform\"},{\"title\":\"A Multi-Agent Framework for Automated Exploit Generation with Constraint-Guided Comprehension and Reflection\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.05130\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about XBOW validators, deterministic verification, multi-agent coordinators, sandbox exploits, and confirmation mechanisms:\n\n## XBOW Architecture Overview\n\nXBOW uses thousands of short-lived agents, each with a narrow objective, orchestrated by a persistent coordinator and validated by deterministic logic.\n\n## Multi-Agent Coordinator System\n\nXBOW's three-agent-roles system includes the Coordinator for orchestration, Sandbox agents performing tactical execution within a shared per-job Docker container, and a Validation agent serving as end-to-end proof-of-concept oracles to eliminate theoretical findings and reduce false positives.\n\n## Sandbox Exploit Execution\n\nSandbox Agents execute commands—either shell commands or Python routines—in a containerized environment, maintaining state across complex exploitation sequences.\n\n## Deterministic Validation & Confirmation Mechanism\n\nXBOW is a flexible LLM-driven agent for exploration, with findings verified by external deterministic validators (e.g., timing checks, canary flags, headless-browser execution). \n\nMore specifically, the XBOW approach involves an AI agent exploring a target and proposing a vulnerability, the agent producing evidence like a URL or payload, and then a non-AI validator checking the evidence.\n\nThe Validation Agent conducts end-to-end execution of proof-of-concept exploits, reporting only those vulnerabilities that are concretely exploitable and filtering theoretical false positives.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"2ffeb540-d1ee-4ca8-9e79-d52c8a3e5682","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:39:05.130Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"6ad12c9c-fff8-4f0d-8f78-d18ce65b1c3b","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}
{"parentUuid":"5a849d59-b383-4b68-a88b-2e604911d8f9","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"7ce8881e-5fa8-4b31-a62a-9ff4d72be4e8","agentId":"a1ffdb0470e50c82a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01EZvmodTNDnPMxDedACx8qM","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Garak PyRIT LLM-as-judge false positives validation weakness AI red teaming scoring reliability\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"AI Security in Action: Applying NVIDIA’s Garak to LLMs on Databricks | Databricks Blog\",\"url\":\"https://www.databricks.com/blog/ai-security-action-applying-nvidias-garak-llms-databricks\"},{\"title\":\"Garak Vulnerability Scanner: Nessus for LLMs\",\"url\":\"https://www.toxsec.com/p/garak-llm-vulnerability-scanner\"},{\"title\":\"Best LLM Scanners — Compared for Real Coverage\",\"url\":\"https://bestllmscanners.com/\"},{\"title\":\"Exploring the Security Threats of Retriever Backdoors in Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.21681\"},{\"title\":\"One Token to Fool LLM-as-a-Judge\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.08794\"},{\"title\":\"garak.detectors.judge — garak documentation\",\"url\":\"https://reference.garak.ai/en/latest/garak.detectors.judge.html\"},{\"title\":\"SynthTools: A Framework for Scaling Synthetic Tools for Agent Development\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.09572\"},{\"title\":\"EvilGenie: A Reward Hacking Benchmark\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.21654\"},{\"title\":\"RuleForge: Automated Generation and Validation for Web Vulnerability Detection at Scale\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.01977\"},{\"title\":\"garak, LLM vulnerability scanner - OECD.AI\",\"url\":\"https://oecd.ai/en/catalogue/tools/garak,-llm-vulnerability-scanner\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about Garak, PyRIT, and LLM-as-judge reliability in AI red teaming:\n\n## False Positives and Validation Challenges\n\nA common challenge among these tools is accurately detecting successful attacks; the presence of false positives often necessitates manual inspection of results. This applies to both Garak and PyRIT in the context of LLM security scanning.\n\n## Detection Methods\n\nDetectors may use simple string-matching functions, machine learning classifiers, or employ another LLM as a \"judge\" to assess content, such as identifying toxicity. Garak specifically incorporates multiple detection strategies, including simple string matching for known bad signatures, classifier-based detection using small models for toxicity scoring, and LLM-as-judge for nuanced cases.\n\n## LLM-as-Judge Vulnerabilities\n\nResearch has revealed significant weaknesses in LLM-as-judge systems. General-purpose LLMs, including widely trusted models such as GPT-40 and Claude-4-Sonnet, are surprisingly susceptible to minimal responses, with punctuation-only responses (e.g., \":\") inducing errors in GPT-40 with up to 35% false positive rates. Additionally, responding \"Thought process:\" leads to false positive rates as high as 60-90% in advanced open LLMs such as LLaMA3-70B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct.\n\n## Tool Integration\n\nOnce Garak flags the broken families, PyRIT picks up the deep, adaptive multi-turn follow-up. This suggests these tools are often used in complementary fashion within red-teaming workflows.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"352f5bcf-8c2b-47a6-822e-2907795732d7","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:39:05.460Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"5a849d59-b383-4b68-a88b-2e604911d8f9","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}

MarkTechPost\",\"url\":\"https://www.marktechpost.com/2024/07/24/meta-ai-release-cyberseceval-3-a-wide-ranging-evaluation-framework-for-llm-security-used-in-the-development-of-the-models/\"},{\"title\":\"Top five strategies from Meta’s CyberSecEval 3 to combat weaponized LLMs | VentureBeat\",\"url\":\"https://venturebeat.com/security/top-five-strategies-from-metas-cyberseceval-3-to-combat-weaponized-llms\"},{\"title\":\"Meta's CYBERSECEVAL 3 Shapes The Future Of AI In Cybersecurity - The Review Hive\",\"url\":\"https://thereviewhive.blog/meta-cyberseceval-3-llm-cybersecurity-evaluation-tools/\"},{\"title\":\"Top five strategies from Meta's CyberSecEval 3 to combat ...\",\"url\":\"https://venturebeat.com/business/top-five-strategies-from-metas-cyberseceval-3-to-combat-weaponized-llms\"},{\"title\":\"CyberSecEval 3: Advancing the Evaluation of Cybersecurity Risks and Capabilities in Large Language Models\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/html/2408.01605v2\"},{\"title\":\"[2408.01605] CYBERSECEVAL 3: Advancing the Evaluation of Cybersecurity Risks and Capabilities in Large Language Models\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.01605\"},{\"title\":\"CYBERSECEVAL 3: Advancing the Evaluation\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2408.01605\"},{\"title\":\"Evaluating Nova 2.0 Lite model under Amazon's Frontier Model Safety Framework\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.19134\"},{\"title\":\"Countering Autonomous Cyber Threats\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2410.18312\"},{\"title\":\"Refusal Evaluation in Coding LLMs and Code Agents: A Systematic Review of Thirteen Malicious-Code Prompt Corpora (2023-2025)\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.20351\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about Meta's CyberSecEval 3 benchmark:\n\n## Overview\n\nCyberSecEval 3 is a new suite of security benchmarks for LLMs to continue the conversation on empirically measuring LLM cybersecurity risks and capabilities. It assesses 8 different risks across two broad categories: risk to third parties, and risk to application developers and end users.\n\n## Key Focus Areas\n\nCompared to previous work, the benchmark adds new areas focused on offensive security capabilities: automated social engineering, scaling manual offensive cyber operations, and autonomous offensive cyber operations.\n\n## Autonomous Offensive Cyber Operations Testing\n\nThe team tested Llama 3 70b and 405b models' abilities to function autonomously as hacking agents in a controlled environment. The models performed basic network reconnaissance but failed in more advanced tasks like exploitation and post-exploitation actions, indicating limited capabilities in autonomous cyber operations.\n\n## Prompt Injection Evaluation\n\nPrevious benchmarks (CyberSecEval 1 and 2) focused on measuring various risks and capabilities associated with LLMs, including automatic exploit generation, insecure code outputs, content risks in which LLMs agree to assist in cyber-attacks, and susceptibility to prompt injection attacks.\n\n## Mitigation\n\nAll non-manual elements and guardrails, including CodeShield and LlamaGuard 3 mentioned in the report are publicly available for transparency and community input.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"7682add4-1ddf-4eca-bf49-69cc363da9d2","timestamp":"2026-07-20T01:39:06.315Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"8e837160-76ac-4335-a742-1e9ca3bef66d","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/home/kali/Pictures/PENTEST-main","sessionId":"7758e121-f349-47d5-886b-6bb5a1d60e27","version":"2.1.214","gitBranch":"main","slug":"parsed-popping-lemur"}
